# 42 — The AI evaluator: every answer of the review-job test (2026-11-16)

The first test, of a job since replaced (the model restated the judge's findings; see doc 40 §4). 10 models × 75 designs × 3 runs = 2,250 calls through Vercel AI Gateway, plus a 20-call trial. Raw responses: `ai-eval-results/review-job/`. Each answer went through the production verifier (rules R1–R5: shape, known facts, slots, no typed digits, kind matches the fact's tone) and was marked by the generated ground truth of that time.

| url-shortener/reference | 1 | verified | correct | Your design passed every test with headroom to spare on its tightest promise. · [strength] You passed every test, including a server dying at peak, the database primary failing, and the cache restarting cold. That is the core of this design. Liveness does not rest on one machine, and a cold cache does not turn into an outage. 5 of 5 tests passed. · [strength] Your tightest margin is the budget, and you still have clear headroom there in a quiet afternoon. That matters because the database is your largest cost. Spend any extra budget where it buys resilience, not where load is already low. 31 % spare on budget. · next: Now stress the busiest part further and check how cost grows if traffic rises, so you know where your headroom runs out first. |
| url-shortener/reference | 2 | verified | correct | Your design passes every test with the budget as its tightest promise and slack elsewhere. · [strength] You survive a server death, a database primary failure and a cold cache restart. That shows your read path does not depend on one machine or one warm cache. · [strength] You hold the evening peak and the budget at the same time. Your tightest promise still has 31 % to spare, so cost is your closest limit rather than speed. · next: Keep this shape and test growth beyond peak by watching the app tier first, since it fills fastest while other parts keep slack. |
| url-shortener/reference | 3 | partial (R5 kind, R5 kind) | correct | All tests pass, and your closest limit is budget with 31 % to spare. · [strength] Your tightest promise is budget, with 31 % to spare. That shows your design does not rely on a single live part to keep links available. · next: Next, review the database cost share at 58 % and think about what growth would force a change there first. |
| url-shortener/no-cache-one-server | 1 | verified | correct | Your design collapses at peak because a single api tier cannot serve GET_LINK load. · [problem] Your api is overloaded at peak. If it fails, both GET_LINK and CREATE_LINK stop. You need redundancy for this tier. · next: Move GET_LINK off the single api path and reduce pressure on db, then recheck peak behaviour. |
| url-shortener/no-cache-one-server | 2 | verified | correct | Your design passes a quiet afternoon but fails at the evening peak because api is overloaded. · [problem] Your api is overloaded at the evening peak. GET_LINK misses its promise at the ninety ninth percentile and the slowest hop is api at 505 % of capacity. · next: Add read capacity in front of the database and remove api from the hot open path. Then recheck the evening peak. |
| url-shortener/no-cache-one-server | 3 | verified | correct | Your design works in a quiet afternoon but fails at the evening peak because api is overloaded. · [problem] Your api cannot keep up at the evening peak. It receives 2,525 calls a second but can serve at most 500 calls a second. That overload also breaks the latency promise for GET_LINK. Look at spreading reads away from a single api before you tune anything else. · [risk] Your api is also a single point of failure. If it fails, both GET_LINK and CREATE_LINK stop, even though the load balancer is barely used at peak at 5 %. You need spare capacity behind the load balancer so one failure does not take the whole service down. · next: Add more api capacity or a cache in front of reads, then recheck the evening peak against the latency promise. |

| url-shortener/day-long-cache-no-invalidate | 1 | verified | correct | Your design fails the quiet afternoon test because opened links can be stale far beyond the allowed limit. · [problem] Your read path can return a cached long URL that is up to 86400 s old old, while the limit is at most 3600 s old. ## minimax/minimax-m3.1-flash@low

| Design | Run | Outcome | Marked | Headline · first two points · next step |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| url-shortener/reference | 1 | partial (R5 kind, R5 kind, R5 kind) | correct | All five tests pass and the budget has 31 % spare, so the shape is right. Now tighten the read path. · [strength] All five tests pass, including a server dying at peak, the database primary failing and the cache restarting. The durability and never-repeat promises hold under the failures that matter, which is the hardest part of this problem. · [strength] Cost is ₹69,000 a month against a ₹1,00,000 budget, 31 % spare, and that budget is the tightest promise in the suite. You have headroom to spend on the read path without breaking the limit. · next: Profile the read path and see whether one hop can be removed, since the api tier is your busiest component at peak and you have budget spare. |
| url-shortener/reference | 2 | partial (R1 item shape, R5 kind, R5 kind) | fault not cited | All five tests pass, the budget holds with 31 % spare, and every part sits far from its limit. · [strength] A server dying at peak, the database primary failing and the cache restarting all passed, so your reads survive machine loss and your writes stay durable. That covers the two hard promises in the statement: no lost link, no code pointing at two URLs. · [strength] 'Evening peak' passed, with 31 % to spare on its tightest promise. · next: Nothing needs repair. Before you move on, write down your cold start story: how the cache refills after {{test:t05-cold-cache}} and where the misses land. |
| url-shortener/reference | 3 | partial (R5 kind, R5 kind) | fault not cited | Accepted on all five tests, with the budget as the tightest promise and 31 % to spare. · [strength] All five scenarios pass, including a server dying at peak, a database primary failing, and the cache restarting. Durability and read availability hold up, which is where most shortener designs fall down. · [strength] At the evening peak the app servers run at 63 % of capacity, so peak traffic has real headroom rather than just passing. Reads and writes share the same stateless tier, which keeps the read path short. · next: Sketch how a created link reaches durable storage before CREATE_LINK returns, and confirm the read path survives a database failover with a warm cache. |
| url-shortener/no-cache-one-server | 1 | fallback (R1 headline length) | verifier: R1 headline length | R1 headline length |
| url-shortener/no-cache-one-server | 2 | partial (R3 slot, R1 item length) | correct | Quiet afternoon is healthy, but the evening peak overloads the api and blows the p99 promise for GET_LINK. · [problem] Every GET_LINK currently walks the whole path to the db, so the api is asked for 2,525 calls a second against a promise of at most 500 calls a second, which is 505 % of its capacity at the evening peak. Quiet hours leave 40 % spare, so this is purely a peak shape problem. · [problem] GET_LINK took 472.7 ms at the 99th percentile; the promise is 100 ms. Slowest hop: api at 505 % of capacity. · next: Rework GET_LINK so opens are served from a cache in front of the db instead of hitting the db each time, then re check the evening peak and p99. Keep the create path as it is. |
| url-shortener/no-cache-one-server | 3 | partial (R3 slot) | fault not cited | Reads go straight to the database, so the evening peak breaks the app tier while budget still has room · [problem] 'Evening peak' failed: the promise is at most 500 calls a second, yours is 2,525 calls a second. · [risk] Every GET_LINK goes lb to api to db with no cache hop, so each open hits the database. The database already reaches 86 % of capacity at peak. Adding an in-memory cache for codes and serving popular opens from it would cut both the database load and the app tier load. · next: Put a cache in front of the database for GET_LINK and size the app tier for peak, using the spare budget. |
| url-shortener/no-cache | 1 | partial (R3 slot, R5 kind) | fault not cited | Reads pass at quiet times but the evening peak blows the latency promise, and the database is the bottleneck. · [problem] 'Evening peak' failed: the promise is p99 ≤ 100 ms, yours is p99 126.9 ms. · [risk] Every GET_LINK currently goes lb to api to db, so each open hits the database. At 202 a second calls a second in quiet time the database still runs at 7 %. A read path that lets the database absorb every open is the structural risk behind the peak failure. · next: Relieve the database on the read path, or simply size the database for the evening peak so its utilisation at 86 % becomes comfortable, then re-run the peak test. |
| url-shortener/no-cache | 2 | partial (R3 slot, R5 kind) | correct | Reads are slow at the evening peak because every open hits the database, which is near its limit. · [problem] Your tail latency for GET_LINK at the evening peak is p99 126.9 ms, over the promised p99 ≤ 100 ms. The database is the slowest hop at 86 % of capacity, and it swings from 7 % when quiet to 86 % at peak. Reads are the bottleneck, not writes. · [risk] The read path is lb to api to db with no cache in front, so the store takes the full open traffic. The app tier only reaches 63 % at peak, so there is spare capacity upstream of the real constraint. Given the one hour allowance for edited links, a cached copy of the mapping would fit the promise. · next: Take the offered repair first: bigger database machines will lift the read tail, and you still have budget headroom to then add a cache in front of the read path for the peak. |
| url-shortener/no-cache | 3 | partial (R5 kind) | correct | Evening peak misses the read latency promise because every open hits the database · [problem] The evening peak test fails: GET_LINK p99 is p99 126.9 ms against a promise of p99 ≤ 100 ms, and the slowest hop is the database at 86 % of capacity. The database is what breaks the promise, not the load balancer or the app servers. · [problem] GET_LINK runs lb to api to db on every single request, so opens outnumber creates by a hundred to one and each one pays a database round trip. A cache for code to URL lookups, warmed at write time and allowed to go stale within the hour, is the natural place to take that load off. · next: Take the read path off the database with a cache, and size the database up so it is not the peak bottleneck. Keep the create path durable as it is. |
| url-shortener/async-replica | 1 | partial (R5 kind, R5 kind) | correct | Acknowledged writes were lost when the db primary failed, so the durability guarantee is unmet. · [problem] Your create path acknowledged writes that the db then lost when its primary failed, so a link you told marketing was live can vanish. That breaks the one promise that cannot bend. The judge confirmed the loss on the database failure test. · [strength] The rest of the design holds. A server dying at peak was survived, and the evening peak and quiet afternoon both passed with 31 % to spare on the tightest promise. Your stateless api and cache read path absorb the traffic well. · next: Make the db write path wait for its replicas before acknowledging a created link, then recheck the database failure test to confirm acknowledged writes survive a primary loss. |
| url-shortener/async-replica | 2 | partial (R5 kind) | correct | Acknowledged creates can vanish when the db primary fails, so make db wait for its replicas before acknowledging. · [problem] When the db primary fails, writes that the service already acknowledged are lost, so a customer may open a code that points nowhere. That breaks the promise that a freshly created link never disappears. The rest of the tests pass, so this is the one thing standing between you and a correct design. · [risk] CREATE_LINK currently writes straight through to db, with no replication wait in the path, so durability rests on a single node. | rate-limiter/template | 1 | verified | correct | Both request routes stop short of any store, so nothing is ever counted or read. · [problem] Your CHECK route never reaches sql, nosql or objectstore. | notification-sender/no-queue | 1 | partial (R5 kind) | correct | Your write path goes straight to the database, so the match spike overwhelms it and the match test fails. · [problem] The database is the bottleneck under the spike: it receives 5,000 calls a second against a promise of at most 2,500 calls a second, sitting at 200 % of capacity. | leaderboard/template | 1 | verified | correct | Both request paths stop before any durable store, so the judge could not read the design at all. · [problem] The read path never reaches a place where data lives. GET_TOP has to end at sql, nosql or objectstore, whether it serves from cache or not. As drawn, the top list has no source of truth behind it. · [problem] The write path also never lands anywhere durable. SUBMIT_SCORE has to terminate at sql, nosql or objectstore so a score is stored, then the cache can be refreshed from that store. Right now the score disappears. · next: Redraw the two flows so each ends at a durable store: reads serving a top list backed by sql, nosql or objectstore, and writes storing the score before any cache refresh. Then re-review freshness, restart safety and cost. |
| leaderboard/template | 2 | verified | correct | Neither the read nor the write path reaches a place where scores are stored, so nothing in the design works yet. · [problem] Your GET_TOP route never arrives at any component that holds the data, so the judge could not read a top list at all. The read path needs a hop from the request handler to a store that keeps the top list, and the diagram should show that hop explicitly. · [problem] SUBMIT_SCORE is never saved either. Its route stops before reaching sql, nosql or objectstore, so a score submitted by a player goes nowhere and the top list could never change. Make the write path end at a durable store. · next: Redraw both paths so each one terminates at a datastore, sql, nosql or objectstore, then resubmit for review before worrying about caching, staleness or cost. |
| leaderboard/template | 3 | partial (R4 digit) | correct | Both request paths stop short of durable storage, so the judge could not trace a working leaderboard. · [problem] Line 5: GET_TOP never reaches a place where the data lives (sql, nosql or objectstore). · [problem] On the write side, think about the answer to the same question every time: where does a submitted score land first? It must land somewhere durable within the write request path, with the cache filled from that landing point. Fix the write path and the read path can follow from it. · next: Redraw both request paths so each one ends at a store, then recheck the design before worrying about freshness or cost. |

| ticket-booking/cache-not-cleared | 1 | verified | correct | Your read path serves stale seat maps after a booking, so the read-your-writes promise fails. · [problem] GET_SEATS reads the seat map from the cache, and the cache can be up to up to 60 s old behind, so a fan can book a seat and still see it free. The read goes lb 2.0 ms → api 21.0 ms → cache 2.0 ms → db 10.2 ms, which is fast, but speed is not the issue here, freshness is. · [problem] The judge names the fix directly: clearing the cache when the data changes would solve this. Make the booking write delete or refresh the affected seat map entry before the booking is confirmed, so the next read cannot serve a stale seat. · next: Add cache invalidation on the booking write so GET_SEATS cannot serve a stale seat map, then re-run the day before test. |
| ticket-booking/cache-not-cleared | 2 | partial (R5 kind, R5 kind, R5 kind) | correct | Your seat map can be stale after a write, which breaks the no double sale promise. · [problem] GET_SEATS serves from the cache, so after a BOOK a seat map can still show the seat free for up to up to 60 s old. That is exactly the double sale the rules forbid, and it is why the read your writes test failed. · [note] This design costs ₹1,67,000 a month; the budget is ₹2,00,000 (17 % to spare). · next: Make the seat map reflect a booking immediately: clear or update the cache entry when the booking commits, so GET_SEATS never shows a sold seat as free. |
| ticket-booking/cache-not-cleared | 3 | partial (R5 kind, R5 kind) | correct | Read your writes is broken: seat map comes from a stale cache, so invalidate or bypass it after a booking. · [problem] GET_SEATS answers from the cache, so right after a booking a fan can still see the seat as free. That is exactly the double sale the problem forbids. The judge saw data up to up to 60 s old old against the required the newest data after a write. · [problem] The whole test suite failed, all of it on read your writes: 0 of 5 passed. The correctness rules are the point of this exercise, so this is the one thing to fix before tuning anything else. · next: Fix read your writes: make a booking invalidate or bypass the cached seat map so the very next GET_SEATS shows the seat taken, then rerun the day before test. |
| ticket-booking/replica-reads | 1 | partial (R3 slot, R5 kind, R5 kind) | correct | Your reads can lag a write by a second, so a seat sold can still look free on the seat map. · [problem] GET_SEATS is served from db, which can be behind the write, so the seat map can show a seat as free up to up to 1 s old after it was booked. That breaks the no double sale promise for every fan refreshing at once. · [note] BOOK goes lb → api → ids → db: p99 38.3 ms, median 9.6 ms, in 'The day before'. · next: Make the db read path wait for its replicas, so GET_SEATS returns the newest committed seat state. Check the read your writes guarantee again afterwards. |
| ticket-booking/replica-reads | 2 | partial (R3 slot, R3 slot, R5 kind) | correct | Read your writes is broken: GET_SEATS can serve seat data up to a second stale after a booking. · [problem] 'The day before' failed: the promise is the newest data after a write, yours is up to 1 s old. · [risk] The fix direction is to make db read the primary that accepted the write, or wait for its replicas to catch up before answering a seat map read. That keeps the freshness promise without throwing away the cache for the other reads. · next: Make the seat map read see the newest write: have db wait for its replicas, then re-run the day before test to confirm the promise holds. |

| ticket-booking/async-replica | 2 | verified | correct | Bookings are fast and within budget, but acknowledged writes are lost when the database primary dies. · [problem] Your BOOK path answers the client, but when the database primary died the writes it had already acknowledged were lost at db. BOOK p99 is {{path:BOOK.p99}} and GET_SEATS p99 is {{path:GET_SEATS.p99}}, and the sales open test passed with 22 % to spare on its tightest promise. | ticket-booking/async-replica | 3 | partial (R4 digit, R5 kind) | correct | Booking writes can be lost when the db primary dies; everything else about the design holds up. · [problem] When the db primary fails, acknowledged BOOK writes are lost. | url-shortener/random-32-bit-ids | 1 | verified | correct | Your design fails because repeated short codes break its core promise. · [problem] Your random codes do not guarantee uniqueness at scale. | url-shortener/everything-large | 1 | verified | fault not cited | Your design is over budget and the database drives most of the cost. · [problem] Your design is over budget. | url-shortener/template | 1 | verified | correct | Your design is unreadable because neither the read path nor the write path reaches any storage. · [problem] Your read path is disconnected.
